Title: Finite-Time Concentration and Convergence Rates for Projected Two-Time-Scale Stochastic Approximation with Markov Noise

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.34791v1

Published Time: Tue, 29 Sep 2026 03:45:29 GMT

Number of Pages: 108

Markdown Content:
# Finite-Time Concentration and Convergence Rates for Projected Two-Time-Scale Stochastic Approximation with Markov Noise 

Rahul Singh 1, Vivek S. Borkar 2, and Eric Moulines 1,3 1Laboratoire de Recherche de l’EPITA, Paris, France 

> 2

Indian Institute of Technology Bombay 

> 3

Mohamed bin Zayed University of Artificial Intelligence, Abu Dhabi, United Arab Emirates 

Abstract 

We study projected two-time-scale stochastic approximation algorithms driven by a con-trolled Markov chain. The fast recursion is governed by an averaged contractive map, while the slow recursion is constrained to a compact convex polyhedron through a projection step. The limiting slow dynamics are described by a projected differential equation, whose vector field may be discontinuous on the boundary of the constraint set. This feature prevents a direct appli-cation of standard finite-time stochastic approximation arguments based on Lipschitz limiting vector fields. We establish explicit blockwise high-probability bounds showing that the fast iterate tracks the moving fixed point x⋆(θn) of the averaged fast recursion and that the step embedding of the slow iterate tracks the associated projected ODE. The bounds separate fast and slow martingale deviations, Poisson residuals generated by Markovian sampling, and the deterministic two-time-scale bias. Under a Lipschitz Lyapunov function satisfying a uniform one-block decrease condition for the projected ODE, these estimates yield almost-sure convergence of the last-iterate Lyapunov error and explicit rates when the decrease modulus has a power lower bound near zero. Uniform one-block contraction is the linear-decrease special case. A uniform decrease condition for a Lipschitz Lyapunov function over fixed time intervals yields almost-sure convergence of the slow Lyapunov error, with explicit last-iterate rates when the decrease admits a power lower bound near zero. Under uniform Lyapunov contraction, suitably chosen polynomial step sizes give joint fast-tracking and slow Lyapunov-error exponents arbitrarily close to 1 /3, while a slow step size of order 1 /n attains the fast-tracking exponent 1 /3. When the reduced slow update map is also a Euclidean contraction, logarithmically separated step sizes improve the joint rate to Oa.s.(n−1/2 log n), including when the equilibrium lies on the boundary. The same rate holds under a distinct geometric condition: the slow equilibrium set is a strictly attracting face of a box, and the fast equilibrium is constant on that face. We apply the results to an actor–critic algorithm, obtaining an almost-sure value-gap rate of 

O(n−1 log n) relative to the optimum within the constrained policy class. Further applications include projected TD(0) and projected stochastic gradient descent. We also extend the analysis to projection of the fast recursion under Euclidean contractivity. 

Keywords: Stochastic approximation, two-time-scale methods, projected dynamical systems, con-trolled Markov chains, Skorokhod map, finite-time concentration, reinforcement learning 1arXiv:2609.34791v1 [math.OC] 28 Sep 2026 1 Introduction 

Stochastic approximation algorithms are iterative schemes for solving fixed-point, root-finding, and optimization problems when only noisy observations of the underlying operator are available. They play a central role in stochastic control, reinforcement learning, simulation-based optimization, and adaptive systems. Many modern applications involve coupled recursions evolving on different time scales, with one component adapting rapidly and another changing more slowly. In addition, constraints on the iterates are often enforced through projection steps. This paper studies finite-time behavior of a projected two-time-scale stochastic approximation scheme driven by a controlled Markov chain. The algorithm consists of a fast recursion xn and a slow projected recursion θn. The slow iterate is projected onto a compact convex polyhedron Θ, while the transition probabilities of the controlled Markov chain are determined by the current value of θn. Because the slow variable changes on a smaller time scale, the fast recursion is expected to equilibrate near the fixed point x⋆(θn) of the averaged fast-time-scale map. We make this intuition quantitative by proving high-probability bounds on the tracking error xn − x⋆(θn). The slow recursion is then compared with the solution of an associated projected ODE. This comparison is nonstandard because the projection may make the limiting vector field discontinuous on the boundary of Θ. We overcome this difficulty by representing each projected path through the Skorokhod map. The Lipschitz property of this map then allows us to control differences between projected paths by differences between their unconstrained driving paths. This yields finite-time concentration bounds for the slow iterate around the projected ODE trajectory. If the associated projected ODE admits a Lipschitz Lyapunov function satisfying a uniform one-block decrease condition along its trajectories, these bounds imply almost-sure convergence of the last-iterate Lyapunov error to zero. Explicit rates follow when the decrease modulus has a power lower bound near zero, with uniform one-block contraction as the linear-decrease special case. Consider the coupled stochastic recursions 

xn+1 = xn + αn[h(Yn, x n, θ n) − xn + Mn+1 ], (1.1)

Under uniform one-block contraction and b < 1, Corollary 8.1 gives joint fast-tracking and slow Lyapunov-error rates with polynomial exponent arbitrarily close to 1 /3, up to a √log( N0 + n) factor. At the harmonic endpoint b = 1, for which βn = ( N0 + n)−1, taking a = 2 /3 gives ∥xn − x⋆(θn)∥ = Oa.s.(( N0 + n)−1/3√log( N0 + n)). The polynomial exponent governing the slow Lyapunov error is then min {λT , 1/3}, with the precise logarithmic factors given in the same corollary; here λT is defined in (8.7). More generally, suppose that the decrease in the Lyapunov function over one fixed block of projected-ODE time is bounded below by a positive constant times its value at the start of the block raised to a power pJ > 1, whenever that value is sufficiently small. whereas its harmonic-endpoint rate is Oa.s.({log( N0 + n)}−1/(pJ −1) ). In Appendices S.1–S.3 of the Online Companion, we specialize the framework to the case where the fast recursion is absent, obtain sharper rate bounds for the remaining projected recursion, and give applications to projected TD(0) and projected SGD. Additional equilibrium geometry improves these polynomial exponents. If the slow equilibrium set is a strictly attracting box face and the fast equilibrium is constant on that face, Proposition 8.1 gives a joint rate Oa.s.(n−1/2 log n) with αn = A log( N0 + n)/(N0 + n) and βn = B/ (N0 + n). Actor– critic algorithm from reinforcement learning satisfies these conditions and also achieves a value-gap rate of Oa.s.(n−1 log n). The projection step introduces a difficulty that is absent in standard finite-time analyses of stochastic approximation. The limiting dynamics are described by a projected differential equa-tion, whose vector field need not be continuous at the boundary of the constraint set. Consequently, standard perturbation arguments for stochastic approximation with Lipschitz limiting vector fields do not directly apply. We therefore follow the ODE method (Borkar [3]): on each finite block of accumulated slow time, we compare the slow step embedding with the projected-ODE solution ini-tialized at the block entrance. We make this projected-ODE comparison quantitative: the Lipschitz continuity of the Skorokhod map on finite time intervals yields explicit path-tracking concentra-tion bounds, and a separate one-block Lyapunov decrease condition converts those bounds into last-iterate rates, with contraction as its linear special case. 

1.1 Contributions 

Our main contributions are as follows. (i) We establish explicit finite-time high-probability concentration bounds showing that the fast iterate xn tracks the moving fixed point x⋆(θn) in projected two-time-scale stochastic approx-imation driven by an iterate-controlled finite-state Markov chain. Only the averaged fast map is assumed contractive; no contraction is imposed on the slow recursion, which is pro-jected onto the compact convex polyhedron Θ. The bounds separate the effects of martingale noise, Markovian sampling, movement of the fast equilibrium, and time-scale separation; see Proposition 6.1. (ii) We prove a finite-horizon supremum-norm concentration bound for the slow iterates around the associated projected-ODE trajectory. We use the Skorokhod map to handle the possible discontinuity of the projected vector field at the boundary; see Theorem 7.1. 3(iii) For the slow iterates, we prove almost-sure convergence to the equilibrium set of the associ-ated projected ODE, assuming that this ODE admits a Lipschitz Lyapunov function that is nonincreasing along trajectories and satisfies a uniform decrease condition over a fixed time horizon T > 0. The horizon can be chosen to establish this decrease, without requiring con-traction or nonexpansiveness of the reduced slow update map. This goes beyond the reduced slow-map contraction assumptions of Chandak [6], Chandak et al. [9] and the nonexpansive-ness assumption of Chandak [5]. The actor–critic application in Section 9 illustrates this distinction: its reduced slow update map need not be nonexpansive, while its projected ODE reaches the restricted-optimal equilibrium face in uniformly bounded time and therefore sat-isfies one-block Lyapunov contraction for a sufficiently large T . To quantify this convergence, we assume that, for sufficiently small Lyapunov errors, the decrease over time T is bounded below by a positive constant times a power of the current Lyapunov error. This yields ex-plicit optimized rates, with one-block Lyapunov contraction as the linear-decrease special case. Under this contraction condition and polynomial step sizes, the joint fast-tracking and slow Lyapunov-error exponent can be taken arbitrarily close to 1 /3; see Theorems 8.2 and 8.4 and Corollaries 8.1 and 8.2. When the slow equilibrium set is a face of a box constraint, the averaged slow drift points strictly outward in each coordinate fixed on that face, and x⋆(θ) is constant on the face, Proposition 8.1 gives the joint rate Oa.s.(n−1/2 log n) with logarithmically separated step sizes. (iv) We apply our results to an actor–critic algorithm in which the current policy controls the Markov sampling process. With logarithmically separated step sizes, the actor value gap rela-tive to the optimum within the constrained policy class is Oa.s.(n−1 log n), and the critic error is Oa.s.(n−1/2 log n). The Online Companion contains the proof and applications to projected TD(0) and projected SGD. It also gives a saddle-point application in Appendix S.18, including examples with vanishing curvature and rotational coupling from a bilinear term. These ex-amples fail both nonexpansiveness of the reduced forward map in any fixed norm and uniform quadratic dissipation, but their projected ODEs satisfy a power Lyapunov decrease condition; see Remark S.18.1. Finally, we extend the analysis to algorithms that project the fast iterate onto a compact convex polyhedron at every step. This makes the fast iterates bounded by construction, removing Assumption 3.7, while retaining contraction of the averaged fast map in the Euclidean norm and bounded noise before projection. 

# 2 Related work 

Representative finite-time results include mean-square, and high-probability concentration bounds due to Borkar [2], Chandak et al. [8], Chen et al. [10, 11]. In two-time-scale stochastic approxi-mation (TTSA), one first studies the fast averaged dynamics with the slow variable frozen. The corresponding reduced slow dynamics are then obtained by replacing the fast variable with its equi-librium x⋆(θ). Existing analyses often impose stability or contraction both on this frozen-slow fast subsystem and on the resulting equilibrium-reduced slow subsystem; see, for example, Chandak [6]. In the linear setting with i.i.d. noise, Konda and Tsitsiklis [17] characterize the asymptotic covariance of two-time-scale stochastic approximation, and establish asymptotic normality. Dalal et al. [12] derive a concentration bound for linear two-time-scale stochastic approximation, with applications to reinforcement-learning algorithms. Doan [13] prove almost-sure convergence and an O(k−2/3) mean-square bound for nonlinear TTSA under strong-monotonicity assumptions on the frozen-slow fast operator and the equilibrium-reduced slow operator. Chandak et al. [9] ob-tain an O(n−2/3) mean-square rate under arbitrary-norm contractions and Markov noise. Chandak 4[6] obtains O(k−a) mean-square error for the two-time-scale schedule αk ≍ k−a, βk ≍ k−1, for 

a ∈ (1 /2, 1). These rates use contraction on both reduced operators. Chandak [5] allows a non-expansive slow map and a projected fast recursion, with a residual mean-square rate O(k−1/4+ ε), where ε > 0 is arbitrarily small. Classical works on projected stochastic approximation focus on asymptotic convergence, weak convergence, or large-deviation principles; see, for example, Dupuis and Kushner [15], Kushner and Yin [19]. Borowski and Miasojedow [4] repair the gap in Kushner and Yin [19, Theorem 5.2.1] for single-time-scale SA with projection onto a hyperrectangle, proving almost-sure convergence to the stationary set of its projected ODE.

# 3 Problem Formulation 

We study the recursions (1.1)–(1.2) introduced in Section 1. Here xn ∈ Rdx is the fast iterate, 

θn ∈ Θ ⊂ Rdθ is the slow iterate, and Θ is a nonempty compact convex polyhedron. The map proj Θ(·) denotes Euclidean projection onto Θ. The initial slow iterate satisfies θ1 ∈ Θ almost surely. The process {Yn} is a controlled Markov chain taking values in the finite state space Y, and its transition law depends on the current slow iterate θn. The functions h : Y × Rdx × Θ → Rdx

and f : Y × Rdx × Θ → Rdθ define the fast and slow updates, respectively. The processes {Mn} in

h(av .)(x; θ) := ∑

> y∈Y

(3.2) For a fixed θ, h(av .)(x; θ) is the mean fast-update map obtained by freezing the slow parameter and averaging h(y, x, θ ) with respect to the stationary distribution μ(θ) of the resulting Markov chain. Thus, h(av .)(x; θ)−x is the corresponding averaged drift of the fast recursion. There exists γ ∈ [0 , 1) such that 

∥∥∥h(av .)(x; θ) − h(av .)(x′; θ)

∥∥∥ ≤ γ∥x − x′∥, x, x ′ ∈ Rdx , θ ∈ Θ.

The unique fixed point of h(av .)(·; θ) is denoted by x⋆(θ), so that 

h(av .)(x⋆(θ); θ) = x⋆(θ).

6Remark 3.1 (Fast-scale contraction) . Assumption 3.4 imposes a contraction condition only on the averaged fast-time-scale map. No contractivity assumption on a discrete slow update map is needed for the concentration and path-tracking results; the slow dynamics are instead characterized through the projected ODE studied later. The constant γ may be replaced by a parameter-dependent contraction factor γ(θ), provided sup θ∈Θ γ(θ) < 1. The extension to an arbitrary fixed norm for the fast coordinate is recorded in Remark S.5.1 of the Online Companion.

Contraction of the averaged fast map does not by itself keep the fast iterates bounded: in-dividual sampled updates may still produce large excursions. Assumption 3.7 ensures that the martingale increments, Poisson-equation solutions, and drift perturbations used throughout the concentration proof are uniformly bounded. It can be verified by finding a deterministic bounded set that contains the initial iterate and is preserved by the recursion, as illustrated in the Online Companion. Alternatively, projecting the fast iterate onto a compact convex polyhedron at every step ensures boundedness by construction. Appendix S.9 develops this extension, removing the need for Assumption 3.7. Set

The ball of radius Rloc contains every fast iterate and every fixed point x⋆(θ). Unless an alternative schedule is stated explicitly, we use polynomial step sizes with a positive-integer shift N0 ≥ 1: 

αn = 1(n + N0)a , β n = 1(n + N0)b , n ∈ N,

where a ∈ (0 , 1) and b ∈ (0 , 1]. The exponent a corresponds to the fast recursion and b to the slow recursion. Since the slow step size must decay faster, we require a < b. The hyperparameters a, b

satisfy the following.

By construction, the path θ◦ is right-continuous with left limits (càdlàg) and piecewise constant. Fix a slow-time block horizon T > 0. Let n0 be the starting index of the analysis specified in Section 3, with n0 = 1 under the default choice of N0. Define recursively

4.1 Projected dynamical systems 

Following Dupuis and Nagurney [16], let C ⊂ Rd be a nonempty closed convex set, and let proj C

denote Euclidean projection onto C. For z ∈ C and v ∈ Rd, define ΠC (z, v ) := lim 

> ε↓0

proj C (z + εv ) − zε . (4.1) For ε > 0, also define ΠC,ε (z, v ) := proj C (z + εv ) − zε . (4.2) We omit the set subscript when it is clear from the context. 

Definition 4.1 (Dupuis and Nagurney [16]) . Let F : C → Rd. A function z : [0 , ∞) → C is a solution of ˙ z = Π C (z, F (z)) if it is absolutely continuous and ˙z(t) = Π C (z(t), F (z(t))) for almost every t ≥ 0. For the slow recursion, we apply these definitions with C = Θ and d = dθ.10 5 Concentration proof sketch and overview 

The concentration arguments are developed in Sections 6 and 7; selected proofs appear in Ap-pendix A and supporting proofs in the Online Companion. Since the fast recursion is contractive after averaging, the first step of the analysis is to show that xn tracks the moving fixed point x⋆(θn)with high probability. This allows the slow recursion to be compared with an auxiliary projected recursion in which xn is replaced by x⋆(θn). After averaging over the controlled Markov chain, the limiting slow dynamics are described by the differential equation ˙z = Π Θ(z(t), f (av .)(z(t); z(t))) , (5.1) where, for θ, θ ′ ∈ Θ, we define 

f (av .)(θ; θ′) := ∑

> y∈Y

μ(θ′)(y)f (y, x ⋆(θ), θ ). (5.2) In f (av .)(θ; θ′), the first argument θ is substituted into both the fixed-point map x⋆(·) and the parameter coordinate of f , while the second argument θ′ determines the stationary distribution used to average over y. We refer to f (av .) as the two-parameter averaged field. Its evaluation is called diagonal when the two arguments agree and off-diagonal otherwise. We first establish a high-probability bound on the fast tracking error ∥xn − x⋆(θn)∥; see Propo-sition 6.1. Using this estimate, we then obtain a concentration bound on the maximum deviation of the embedding of the slow iterates from the projected-ODE trajectory over each finite slow-time block; see Theorem 7.1. For τ ≥ 0, let z(τ )(·) denote the solution to (5.1) satisfying 

z(τ )(0) = θ◦(τ ).

In particular, on the mth block, we compare 

θ(m),◦(r) = θ◦(Tm + r)with z(Tm)(r) for 0 ≤ r ≤ Hm.Our analysis has three main components. First, Section 6 establishes a high-probability tracking bound for the fast iterate xn around the moving equilibrium x⋆(θn). Second, Section 7 compares the step embedding θ◦ with the projected ODE over finite time intervals. Third, Section 8 combines this finite-time tracking estimate with a Lipschitz Lyapunov function satisfying a uniform one-block decrease condition for the projected ODE to obtain almost-sure convergence. Explicit rates follow when, for sufficiently small Lyapunov errors, the decrease over a fixed time horizon is bounded below by a positive constant times a power of the current error. One-block Lyapunov contraction is the linear-decrease special case. The main technical obstacle is the projection. The projected vector field in the limiting ODE may be discontinuous on the boundary of Θ, so standard stochastic-approximation concentration arguments based on Lipschitz ODE fields do not apply directly. We instead use right-continuous step embeddings of the discrete projected recursions and their unconstrained drivers. More pre-cisely, each projected step path is exactly the image of its corresponding unconstrained driving path under the Skorokhod map. The finite-horizon supremum-norm Lipschitz property of that map then controls the difference between two projected paths through the difference between their unconstrained drivers. 11 6 Blockwise concentration of the fast iterate

∥xnm+k − x⋆(θnm+k)∥, 0 ≤ k ≤ Km.

We bound this error through two comparisons: first, between the original fast recursion and a block-restarted averaged recursion; second, between this averaged recursion and the moving equilibrium. Fix m ≥ 0. For 0 ≤ k ≤ Km, write

The Markov noise 

h(Yn, x n, θ n) − h(av .)(xn; θn)is generally not a martingale difference because Yn is Markovian. The Poisson equation is used to decompose this error into a martingale-difference term and a difference between two successive-state evaluations of the Poisson solution. Since the sampling error enters the fast update multiplied by αn, iterating the recursion produces weighted partial sums of the two terms. We control the martingale contribution using a concentration inequality and bound the remaining Poisson residual by summation by parts, using the slow variation of ( xn, θ n).

We now establish a blockwise high-probability comparison between the step embedding of the slow iterate and the projected ODE (5.1). On each block, we introduce an auxiliary projected recursion and decompose the comparison into two stages. First, we compare the original slow recursion with the auxiliary recursion, thereby isolating the fast-tracking error, the martingale noise in the slow recursion, and the Markov noise. Second, we compare the auxiliary recursion with the projected ODE. This second comparison controls the time-grid discretization error and the mismatch arising because the auxiliary recursion evaluates the first and second arguments of the averaged field at its own current state and the original slow state, respectively, whereas the ODE evaluates both arguments at its current state. Throughout this section, “auxiliary recursion” refers to the slow projected recursion introduced below. It is distinct from the block-restarted averaged fast recursion of Section 6. Since the projection operator is nonlinear, both comparisons are carried out through the cor-responding unconstrained driving paths. Bounds on the driving paths are then transferred to the projected paths using the Lipschitz property of the Skorokhod map. Fix m ≥ 0 and use the block notation introduced in Section 4. In particular, for 0 ≤ n ≤ Km,write 

θ(m)

:= θnm+n, x(m)

# 8 Almost-sure convergence and last-iterate rates 

We now convert the localized blockwise projected-ODE tracking estimate of Theorem 7.1 into iteration-wise almost-sure rates. The argument has three steps. First, we choose block-dependent fast- and slow-martingale deviation thresholds whose failure probabilities are summable over the blocks. The Borel–Cantelli lemma then implies that the blockwise tracking estimate holds on every sufficiently large block almost surely. Second, a uniform one-block Lyapunov decrease condition yields a perturbed nonlinear recursion at the block endpoints. Third, we analyze this recursion and use the within-block tracking estimate to transfer the endpoint bound to every discrete iterate. Uniform one-block contraction is the special case in which the Lyapunov value decreases by at least a fixed proportion over each block. Throughout this section, the standing assumptions and Condition 3.1 of Section 3, together with (3.3), remain in force. For s ≥ 0, let Ss denote the time-s flow map of the projected ODE (5.1). The well-posedness of (5.1) is established in Lemma S.15.1. Let EQ Θ denote the set of equilibrium points of the ODE (5.1): 

EQ Θ := 

{

z ∈ Θ : Π Θ(z, f (av .)(z; z)) = 0

}

.

21 Definition 8.1 (Lyapunov function) . Consider the projected ODE (5.1), repeated here for conve-nience: ˙z(t) = Π Θ

(

z(t), f (av .)(z(t); z(t)) 

)

, z(0) = z0 ∈ Θ.

A continuous function J : Θ → R+ is called a Lyapunov function for (5.1) if J(z) = 0 if and only if z ∈ EQ Θ, and if, for every solution z(·) of (5.1), 

J(z(t)) = J(St−s(z(s))) ≤ J(z(s)) , 0 ≤ s ≤ t < ∞.

Thus J is nonincreasing along trajectories of the projected ODE. For any Lyapunov function J as in Definition 8.1, write 

Jmax := max  

> θ∈Θ

J(θ) < ∞,

where finiteness follows from the continuity of J and the compactness of Θ. We use a Lyapunov function to measure convergence to the equilibrium set.

θ(m),◦ and the projected-ODE trajectory z(Tm) started at θnm . We now choose block-dependent thresholds δf and δs that vanish as m → ∞ , while their block-failure probabilities remain summable. Smaller deviation levels sharpen this bound but increase the block-failure probabilities; the loga-rithmic choices below make both levels vanish while keeping those probabilities summable. Assumption 8.2 (Uniform one-block Lyapunov decrease) . Let J be the Lyapunov function in Assumption 8.1. Let T > 0 be the block horizon used in the definition of {nm}. There exists a continuous function 

DJ : [0 , J max ] → R+

J(Ss(θ)) ≤ J(ST (θ)) ≤ J(θ) − D J (J(θ)) , θ ∈ Θ.

A decrease proportional to the current Lyapunov value gives the following contraction condition.

8.1 Faster rates at an attracting equilibrium face 

The general fast-tracking bound accounts for movement of x⋆(θn) as the slow iterate evolves. This contribution can be reduced when the slow equilibrium set is a strictly attracting face of a box and 

x⋆ has the same value at every point of that face. The resulting rates are given next.

A different mechanism yields the n−1/2 polynomial exponent: assume that the reduced slow update map is a Euclidean contraction. Together with logarithmic separation of the two step sizes, this controls the slow error directly, even when its equilibrium lies on the boundary. Unlike Proposi-tion 8.1, the next result permits an arbitrary compact convex polyhedron and does not require an attracting box face or a common fast target along a face. Its contraction assumption is stronger than the one-block Lyapunov condition.

where N0 ≥ 3, α1 < 1, and A log t1 ≥ max {4, B }. Assumption 3.7 is required for this selected schedule. Then

. (8.15) 

The same bound holds for ∥xn − x⋆(θ⋆)∥ and for J(θn) whenever J is Lipschitz and J(θ⋆) = 0 .Proof. See Appendix S.17. The equilibrium drift may be nonzero and outward; neither interiority nor strict complemen-tarity is assumed. Condition (8.13) implies contraction of distance along the projected ODE and is stronger than the one-block Lyapunov contraction used in Theorem 8.4. In the terminology of Chandak [6], “true” two-time-scale stochastic approximation requires 

βn/α n → 0, not specifically polynomial separation. The schedule (8.14) satisfies this condition since 

βn/α n = B/ (A log tn). Under the additional contraction condition (8.13), the bound (8.15) has polynomial exponent 1 /2 for both iterate errors, with only logarithmic separation of the step sizes. This improvement uses the stronger contraction assumption together with the revised schedule and proof; it does not follow from changing the schedule alone. The proof adapts the auxiliary noise-averaging idea of that work to the projected recursion and controlled Markov noise, using a feasible auxiliary sequence to handle boundary equilibria. One setting in which the reduced-map condition can be checked directly is projected gradient descent for a smooth strongly convex objective. If G(θ) = θ − η∇F (θ) and mI ⪯ ∇ 2F (θ) ⪯ LI on Θ, then, for 0 < η < 2/L , the mean-value theorem gives 

> ∥∥∥

G(θ) − G(ϑ) 

> ∥∥∥

≤ max {| 1 − ηm |, |1 − ηL |} ∥ θ − ϑ∥ .

The displayed factor is less than one. Projection onto a compact convex polyhedron preserves this contraction bound, including when the constrained minimizer lies on a boundary face. 27 9 Actor–Critic Application 

Actor–critic provides an example in which the slow iterate controls both the sampling law and the projected update. We first specify the single-trajectory algorithm and its constrained policy class, then state the rate obtained from the attracting-face result. The verification of the assumptions is in the Online Companion; standalone projected TD(0) and projected-SGD applications appear in Appendices S.1 and S.2 there. Consider a finite-state, finite-action Markov decision process (MDP); see, for example, Puterman [21]. Let S and A be its state and action spaces, P (s′ | s, a ) its transition probabilities, and c(s, a )its expected one-step cost. At time n, the current actor parameter determines a policy from which 

An is drawn at state Sn. The environment then produces Sn+1 and a realized one-step cost Cn+1 .Let FMDP

The Online Companion, Appendix S.8, expresses the critic and actor updates in the drift-plus-martingale form of (1.1)–(1.2) and checks the assumptions used in the rate results. Fix 0 < γ disc < 1. For a stationary policy π, its discounted total-cost value is

We analyze an online actor–critic recursion using one observed state–action transition per iteration, motivated by Konda and Borkar [18, Algorithm 3]. In the original asynchronous form, an update associated with a state–action pair uses a step size indexed by that pair’s visit count. Here the critic coordinate for the observed state and the actor coordinate for the observed state–action pair are updated using the global step sizes αn and βn, indexed by the number n of environment transitions. The rate below applies to this global-clock recursion. Assume |Cn+1 | ≤ Cmax . The actor parameter ϑ = ( ϑ(s, a )) s,a consists of state–action logits, also called preference parameters. Appendix S.8 of the Online Companion uses their uniform positive lower bound to establish Lyapunov decrease, identify the restricted-optimal equilibrium face, and show that the projected actor ODE reaches that face in uniformly bounded time. On the face, the critic equilibrium is V ⋆Bac , and each coordinate fixed at an endpoint has strictly outward drift. Hence Proposition 8.1 applies.

This controls the aggregate value Lyapunov gap and the critic tracking error. If V ⋆ is the unre-stricted optimal value, then 

0 ≤ ∑

> s∈S

Consequently, for any prescribed target tolerance εpol > 0, a fixed Bac can be chosen sufficiently large that the displayed restriction bias is at most εpol . The unrestricted aggregate value gap is then at most εpol plus the vanishing term in (9.3) .

# 10 Conclusion 

We obtained finite-time concentration bounds for projected two-time-scale stochastic approxima-tion driven by a controlled Markov chain. On each slow-time block, the bounds compare the fast 29 iterate with its moving equilibrium and the projected slow path with its ODE trajectory. The Skorokhod-map comparison handles boundary discontinuities, while the estimates identify sepa-rately the martingale, Markovian, and time-scale contributions. A one-block Lyapunov decrease gives almost-sure convergence to the slow equilibrium set; power decrease and contraction give explicit last-iterate rates. For polynomial step sizes, the optimized joint exponent under one-block contraction approaches 1 /3. With a strictly attracting equilibrium face and a common fast target, logarithmically separated step sizes give the joint Oa.s.(n−1/2 log n)rate. Euclidean contraction of the reduced slow map gives the same joint order on a general compact convex polyhedron, including boundary equilibria. In actor–critic, the attracting-face result further gives an Oa.s.(n−1 log n) constrained-policy value gap. The Online Companion develops the actor– critic proof, projected TD(0), projected SGD, and fast-coordinate projection. 

# A Selected Proofs of the Main Results 

We give the path comparisons underlying the main concentration bounds and the argument that car-ries Lyapunov control from block endpoints to every iteration. These comprise the two comparisons for the fast recursion, their fast-tracking consequence, the original–auxiliary and auxiliary–ODE comparisons for the slow recursion, and the localized projected-ODE comparison. The step-path Skorokhod representation, supporting Poisson-equation and martingale estimates, and the remain-ing proofs are collected in the Online Companion.

A.7 Proof of Theorem 8.2 

Proof. By the Borel–Cantelli argument in Section 8, almost surely there exists m0 < ∞ such that Grate  

> m

holds for every m ≥ m0. Fix such a sample path and write Um := J(θnm ) and eJm := 

LJ CB,T drate  

> m

. At the block endpoint, (8.1) and the Lipschitz continuity of J, followed by nonincrease of J along the ODE flow, Hm ≥ T , and Assumption 8.2, give 

Um+1 ≤ J(z(Tm)(Hm)) + eJm ≤ Um − D J (Um) + eJm.

This proves (8.2). For nm ≤ n < n m+1 , let rn := t(n) − Tm ∈ [0 , H m]. Then θ(m),◦(rn) = θn, and the within-block tracking estimate gives 

J(θn) ≤ J(z(Tm)(rn)) + eJm ≤ Um + eJm.

This proves (8.3). It remains to prove convergence. If Jmax = 0, the conclusion is immediate. Otherwise, fix 0 < η ≤ 2Jmax and set cη := min u∈[η/ 2,J max ] DJ (u) > 0. The definition of drate  

> m

and Lemmas S.15.4 and S.15.5, in their respective cases, give drate  

> m

→ 0, and hence eJm → 0. Thus, for all sufficiently large m,

eJm ≤ min {cη/2, η/ 2}, Um ≥ η/ 2 =⇒ Um+1 ≤ Um − cη/2.

The sequence must therefore eventually enter [0 , η/ 2). Once it does, the two preceding inequalities show that it remains in [0 , η ]: below η/ 2, its next increase is at most eJm, while on [ η/ 2, η ] it decreases by at least cη/2. Since η > 0 is arbitrary, Um → 0. The within-block estimate and 

eJm → 0 give J(θn) → 0. Finally, compactness of Θ, continuity of J, and J−1({0}) = EQ Θ imply dist( θn, EQ Θ) → 0. 34 Acknowledgments

Guide to the Supplementary Appendix. Appendix S.1 gives a standalone projected on-policy linear TD(0) application and its last-iterate rate. Appendix S.2 gives the projected-SGD application, including distance and objective-gap rates, and Appendix S.3 states the general one-time-scale specializations under contraction and power Lyapunov decrease. Appendix S.4 establishes regularity of the stationary law and fast equilibrium. Appendices S.5– S.7 contain, respectively, the remaining fast-scale proofs and supporting results; the remaining slow-scale martingale, Poisson, and projected-path arguments; and the remaining convergence and rate proofs, including those for the one-time-scale specializations. Appendix S.7 also proves Propo-sition 8.1 and records the harmonic-endpoint sharpness example. The seven selected proofs of the main results appear in Sections A.1– A.7 of the main-paper appendix. Appendix S.5 also records, in Remark S.5.1, the extension of the unprojected fast-scale analysis to an arbitrary fixed fast-coordinate norm. Appendix S.8 verifies the actor–critic application, including controlled-chain coverage, critic contraction, Lyapunov geometry, restriction bias, and improved actor and critic rates. Appendix S.9 gives the optional extension with every-step compact-polyhedral Euclidean projection of the fast iterate, including high-probability tracking and almost-sure rates. Appen-dices S.10–S.12 collect, in order, the auxiliary fast-scale Poisson estimates, the auxiliary slow-scale Poisson estimates, and the strong-monotonicity consequences for projected ODEs. Appendix S.13 contains the Skorokhod-map tools used for projection. Appendix S.14 derives auxiliary results related to step-sizes. Appendix S.15 contains several miscellaneous results, while Appendix S.16 discusses the shifted-step-size and finite-burn-in alternatives. For 1 /2 < b ≤ 1 and λ > 0, define the rate envelope used in the one-time-scale contraction results below (including the projected TD(0) and projected-SGD applications) by 

Rn(b, λ ) :=

(N0 + n)−b/2√log( N0 + n), 12 < b < 1,

(N0 + n)−1/2√log( N0 + n), b = 1 , λ > 12 ,

(N0 + n)−1/2{log( N0 + n)}3/2, b = 1 , λ = 12 ,

(N0 + n)−λ, b = 1 , 0 < λ < 12 .

# S.2 Optimization: Projected Stochastic Gradient Descent 

We consider the constrained convex optimization problem Φ ⋆ := min θ∈Θ Φ( θ), where Θ is a compact convex polyhedron and Φ is continuously differentiable on an open neighborhood of Θ, convex on Θ, and has an LΦ-Lipschitz gradient there. Projected SGD for this problem is 

θn+1 = proj Θ [θn − βnGn+1 ] , βn = ( N0 + n)−b, 12 < b ≤ 1, (S.2.1) Let Fopt  

> n

denote the natural optimization history through time n, including the current iterate and all oracle samples revealed before Gn+1 is sampled. Assume that, for some deterministic Bsgd < ∞,

E[Gn+1 | F opt  

> n

] = ∇Φ( θn), ∥Gn+1 − ∇ Φ( θn)∥ ≤ Bsgd a.s. This is a one-time-scale singleton-state application, so Markov noise is absent. Its limiting projected gradient flow is ˙z = Π Θ (z, −∇ Φ( z)) . (S.2.2) 

Corollary S.2.1 (Almost-sure rates for projected SGD) . Suppose that 1/2 < b ≤ 1 and that Φ

is mΦ-strongly convex on an open neighborhood of Θ, and let θ⋆ be its unique minimizer over Θ.Then, 

∥θn − θ⋆∥ = Oa.s. (Rn(b, m Φ)) , (S.2.3) Φ( θn) − Φ⋆ = Oa.s. (Rn(b, m Φ)) . (S.2.4)

> n

}. Hence (S.2.1) has the one-time-scale form in Proposition S.3.1, with drift −∇ Φ. Taking the Markov state to be a singleton makes Assumptions 3.1, 3.2, and 3.6 au-tomatic. The oracle condition gives the bounded martingale difference required by Assumption 3.3, 40 while g = −∇ Φ is LΦ-Lipschitz on Θ, as required by Assumption 3.5; compactness of Θ also makes this drift bounded. The required flow contraction is verified next. Under strong convexity, 

⟨−∇ Φ( θ) + ∇Φ( θ⋆), θ − θ⋆⟩ ≤ − mΦ ∥θ − θ⋆∥2 .

The first-order optimality condition ⟨∇ Φ( θ⋆), θ −θ⋆⟩ ≥ 0 for every θ ∈ Θ is equivalent to Π Θ(θ⋆, −∇ Φ( θ⋆)) = 0; see Dupuis and Nagurney [16, Lemma 1]. Thus θ⋆ is an equilibrium of (S.2.2). Hence Corollary S.12.1 gives, with S(1)

# S.3 One-time-scale specialization 

Recursions without a fast variable can be embedded in the general scheme by taking the fast coordinate, its update, and its noise identically zero. We state the resulting one-time-scale guarantee as follows.

S.5.5 Arbitrary fast-coordinate norms 

Remark S.5.1 (Choice of norm for the fast coordinate) . The fast-scale arguments in Sections 6– 8 remain valid if the Euclidean norm on Rdx is replaced by any fixed norm ∥·∥ x, provided that the contraction, local Lipschitz, bounded-set, stability, and noise bounds involving the fast coordinate are formulated in that norm. In B(R), the fast-state radius and the norm of h are then interpreted using ∥·∥ x, while the norm of f remains Euclidean. Recall the two norm-equivalence constants introduced in the proof of Lemma 6.2: 

κx = sup 

> ∥u∥∞≤1

∥u∥x , λx = sup 

> ∥u∥x≤1

∥u∥∞ .

46 Thus ∥z∥x ≤ κx ∥z∥∞ and ∥z∥∞ ≤ λx ∥z∥x. The first inequality converts the coordinatewise martingale estimate to ∥·∥ x, while the second converts an x-norm increment bound to a coordi-natewise one. The corresponding fast-martingale event and tail constant are recorded in the proof of Lemma 6.2 in Section S.5.2 of the Online Companion. The slow projection and Skorokhod-map arguments remain Euclidean.

a/2 = b − a, that is, a = 2 b/3, and equals b/3. For 1 /2 < b ≤ 3/4, its supremum is b − 1/2, 51 approached as a ↓ 1/2 (and not attained under the strict constraint). In either case, the supremum over 1 /2 < a < b < 1 is at most 1 /3. For the displayed choice 

b = 1 − 3εrate , a = 2b

Remark S.7.2 (Comparison with recent mean-square bounds) . For every fixed 0 < ε rate < 1/12, Corollary 8.1 gives an almost-sure distance envelope of order n−1/3+ εrate √log n, whose square has trajectory-wise scale n−2/3+2 εrate log n. This observation does not imply a mean-square bound: passing from an almost-sure envelope with a random prefactor to an expectation requires an addi-tional integrability argument. At the level of exponents, the trajectory-wise squared scale agrees, up to logarithmic and arbitrarily small polynomial losses, with the O(n−2/3) mean-square rate for contractive two-time-scale SA with arbitrary-norm contractions and Markov noise in Chandak et al. [9]. Other O(k−a) or O(k−1) mean-square results have been obtained by Chandak [6], Doan [14] under different assumptions or for modified recursions. Because their algorithms, assumptions, and modes of convergence differ, those results are not directly comparable with the almost-sure envelope above. Our result instead provides blockwise high-probability tracking for projected-ODE slow dynamics without assuming a contractive discrete slow update map. The comparison in this remark concerns the linear-decrease branch, which uses uniform one-block contraction of a Lips-chitz Lyapunov function. The nonlinear decrease result gives different exponents and is not being compared here with the cited contractive mean-square bounds.

This uses only the driver estimates just obtained and the vanishing largest step in the block. In particular, it applies to the second schedule without invoking any pure-power rate formula. Lipschitz continuity of J and its one-block decrease therefore give

with a within-block increase bounded by o(1). Since DJ is continuous and strictly positive away from zero, the scalar convergence argument in Theorem 8.2 gives J(θn) → 0. Compactness of Θ and J−1({0}) = EQ Θ then imply dist( θn, EQ Θ) → 0 almost surely. These consistency statements are the only inputs from this step needed for the sharper rates.

∥∥∥h(av .)ac (V ; ϑ) − h(av .)ac (V ′; ϑ)

The critic is bounded: if BV := Cmax /(1 − γdisc ) and ∥Vn∥∞ ≤ BV , then its updated coordinate is a convex combination of Vn(Sn) and Cn+1 + γdisc Vn(Sn+1 ), both in [ −BV , B V ], since 0 < α n ≤ 1. Induction therefore gives sup

∥Vn∥∞ ≤ BV .

We apply Remark S.5.1 with ∥·∥ x = ∥·∥ ∞. Thus κx = λx = 1, and the fast contraction, local Lipschitz, bounded-set, stability, and martingale bounds are interpreted in this norm; the slow coordinate and its projection remain Euclidean. We next verify the localized Lipschitz condition directly for the original update maps. Since the softmax map ϑ 7 → πϑ is continuously differentiable and its Jacobian is continuous, its Jacobian is uniformly bounded on the compact logit box. Moreover, cϑ and Pϑ are linear functions of πϑ. Hence, by the line-segment form of the mean-value theorem, there exist finite constants Lπ, L c, L P > 0such that, for all ϑ, ϑ ′ ∈ Θac ,max

To prove (9.2), observe that full support of the softmax policy gives 

Pϑ(s, s ′) > 0 ⇐⇒ P (s′ | s, a ) > 0 for some a ∈ A .

If ϑk → ϑ, every subsequential limit of dϑk is stationary for Pϑ, by continuity of ϑ 7 → Pϑ. Uniqueness of the stationary distribution therefore gives dϑk → dϑ. Thus ϑ 7 → min s∈S dϑ(s) is continuous and strictly positive. Compactness of Θ ac proves (9.2). Equilibrium set and finite-time convergence of the actor ODE. To verify the uniform one-block Lyapunov contraction condition required by Theorem 8.4, we first identify the equilibrium set of the projected actor ODE and then show that every trajectory reaches this set within a uniform finite time. N ⋆s (ϑ(s, ·)) = Zs(ϑ(s, ·)) {[TϑV ⋆Bac ]( s) − V ⋆Bac (s)} = 0 .

If K⋆s,a > 0 but ϑ(s, a ) > −Bac , lowering only that logit makes N ⋆s strictly negative. Since Zs > 0, this contradicts the preceding restricted Bellman minimum. Likewise, if K⋆s,a < 0 but ϑ(s, a ) < B ac ,raising only that logit makes N ⋆s strictly negative. Hence ϑ ∈ E Bac .Conversely, suppose ϑ ∈ E Bac . The endpoint signs imply, for every feasible η,

> a

The restricted Bellman minimum is zero, so every feasible numerator is nonnegative. Moreover, compactness gives a minimizer ¯ ηs with N ⋆s (¯ ηs) = 0. The preceding comparison, together with the feasibility of ϑ(s, ·), therefore gives N ⋆s (ϑ(s, ·)) = 0. Thus [TϑV ⋆Bac ]( s) − V ⋆Bac (s) = N ⋆s (ϑ(s, ·)) 

Zs(ϑ(s, ·)) = 0 .

Since this holds for every state, V ⋆Bac is the fixed point of Tϑ. Uniqueness of that fixed point gives 

Vϑ = V ⋆Bac , and hence 

JBac (ϑ) = 0 ⇐⇒ ϑ ∈ E Bac . (S.8.7) If ϑ ∈ E Bac , the equality Vϑ = V ⋆Bac gives Ks,a (ϑ) = K⋆s,a , and the endpoint conditions give 

χs,a (ϑ)Ks,a (ϑ) = 0 for every ( s, a ). Conversely, if these products vanish, the residual bound gives 

JBac (ϑ) = 0, so (S.8.7) yields ϑ ∈ E Bac . Since qϑ(s, a ) > 0, the products vanish exactly when the projected actor velocity vanishes. Thus EBac , equivalently the zero set of JBac , is precisely the equilibrium set of the projected actor ODE. If K⋆s,a = 0 for every ( s, a ), then EBac = Θ ac , JBac is identically zero, and we set Thit := 0. The finite-hitting property is then immediate. # S.9 Extension: Projection of the Fast-Scale Iterate 

This section gives an optional extension of the framework in which the fast iterate is projected at every iteration. The main-paper recursion and the results proved for it are not changed. Instead, throughout this section we replace (1.1) by 

xn+1 = proj X (xn + αn{h(Yn, x n, θ n) − xn + Mn+1 }) , x1 ∈ X , (S.9.1) while retaining the projected slow recursion (1.2). Here X ⊂ Rdx is fixed: it does not depend on 

n or on θ. Consequently, xn ∈ X for every n, and the fast-stability Assumption 3.7 is no longer needed. This extension is useful when boundedness of the unprojected fast iterates is not available a priori or would require a separate application-specific stability proof. The main paper assumes such boundedness, whereas the modified recursion enforces it by projecting the fast iterate at every step onto a prescribed compact feasible region. Subject to the Euclidean contraction condition on X and the bounded-preprojection-noise condition stated below, the extension then gives the corresponding high-probability path-tracking and almost-sure last-iterate guarantees. 

S.9.1 Euclidean projection and contraction 

Throughout this section, the fast-coordinate norm is the Euclidean norm. Let X ⊂ Rdx be a nonempty compact convex polyhedron, and write PX := proj X for its Euclidean projection. We impose the averaged-contraction condition in the Euclidean norm and only on X :

S.9.3 Fixed fast-time blocks and the moving-parameter comparison 

The projected-fast comparison cannot be run over an entire slow-time block by a direct Gronwall argument: the fast-time duration of such a block is of order αnm /β nm , which diverges. We therefore partition each slow block into subblocks having a fixed duration in the fast clock. Fix a fast-time horizon Tx > 0. # S.12 Strong monotonicity and exponential stability of projected ODEs 

Definition S.12.1. Let K ⊂ Rd be a nonempty closed convex set and let F : K → Rd. Define ΠK (x, v ) := lim 

> h↓0

proj K (x + hv ) − xh , x ∈ K, v ∈ Rd,

and consider the projected dynamical system ˙x = Π K (x, F (x)) .

The vector field F is strongly monotone at x⋆ ∈ K if there exists μsm > 0 such that 

⟨F (x) − F (x⋆), x − x⋆⟩ ≤ − μsm ∥x − x⋆∥2 , x ∈ K. 

84 Theorem S.12.1. Let K be a nonempty compact convex polyhedron and let F : K → Rd be Lipschitz. Suppose that x⋆ ∈ K is an equilibrium of 

˙x = Π K (x, F (x)) 

and that F is strongly monotone at x⋆, in the sense of Definition S.12.1, with constant μsm > 0.Then the projected ODE is well posed and every solution satisfies 

∥x(t) − x⋆∥ ≤ e−μsm t ∥x(0) − x⋆∥ , t ≥ 0.

In particular, x⋆ is the unique globally exponentially stable equilibrium. Proof. Set 

Fext (z) := F (proj K z), z ∈ Rd.

Since Euclidean projection is nonexpansive, Fext is globally Lipschitz; since K is compact, it is also bounded. Thus Assumption 1 of Dupuis and Nagurney [16] holds. Taking xn 

> 0

= x0, bni = Fext , and 

ani = 1 /n in their Theorem 3 gives existence, while their Theorem 2 gives uniqueness. Since x⋆ is an equilibrium, it is a constant solution. Repeating the comparison calculation leading to equation (15) in the proof of Dupuis and Nagurney [16, Theorem 2], with their drift 

b = Fext and with the two solutions x(·) and x⋆, gives, for almost every t ≥ 0, 12ddt ∥x(t) − x⋆∥2 ≤ ⟨ F (x(t)) − F (x⋆), x (t) − x⋆⟩ ≤ − μsm ∥x(t) − x⋆∥2 .

Gronwall’s inequality gives 

∥x(t) − x⋆∥ ≤ e−μsm t ∥x(0) − x⋆∥ , t ≥ 0.

Applying this estimate to any constant equilibrium trajectory also proves that x⋆ is the unique equilibrium. 

Corollary S.12.1 (Lyapunov and one-block consequences) . Under the hypotheses of Theorem S.12.1, let SFt denote the projected-ODE flow and define 

J(x) := ∥x − x⋆∥ .

Then J is a 1-Lipschitz Lyapunov function and, for every T > 0,

J(SFT (x)) ≤ ρT J(x), ρT := e−μsm T ∈ (0 , 1) , x ∈ K. 

Thus the uniform one-block Lyapunov contraction condition holds for every T > 0.

Lemma S.12.1. Let K be a nonempty compact convex polyhedron. Consider the projected ODE 

˙x = Π K

> (

x, Tctr (x) − x),

where Tctr : K → K is a contraction with contraction factor γ ∈ [0 , 1) . If x⋆ is an equilibrium, then 

Tctr (x⋆) = x⋆, the vector field 

Fctr (x) := Tctr (x) − x

is strongly monotone at x⋆, and Corollary S.12.1 applies. 

85 Proof. Since Tctr (x⋆) ∈ K, the vector 

Tctr (x⋆) − x⋆

belongs to the tangent cone of K at x⋆. Because x⋆ is an equilibrium, ΠK

> (

x⋆, Tctr (x⋆) − x⋆) = 0 .

It follows that 

Tctr (x⋆) = x⋆.

For every x ∈ K,

⟨Fctr (x) − Fctr (x⋆), x − x⋆⟩ = ⟨T ctr (x) − T ctr (x⋆), x − x⋆⟩ − ∥ x − x⋆∥2

≤ ∥T ctr (x) − T ctr (x⋆)∥ ∥ x − x⋆∥ − ∥ x − x⋆∥2

≤ − (1 − γ) ∥x − x⋆∥2 .

Thus Fctr is strongly monotone at x⋆, with constant 1 − γ. The conclusion now follows from Corollary S.12.1. 

# S.13 Skorokhod problem 

We recall the Skorokhod problem and the Lipschitz property of its solution map. This property is used to convert bounds between unconstrained driving paths into bounds between the corresponding projected paths. Let D([0 , ∞); Rd) denote the space of right-continuous functions with left limits from [0 , ∞) to 

Rd. If υ : [0 , ∞) → Rd, let |υ|(T ) denote its total variation on [0 , T ]. Let DBV ([0 , ∞); Rd) be the set of all υ ∈ D([0 , ∞); Rd) such that υ has bounded variation on every compact interval. Let ∂Θdenote the boundary of Θ. For x ∈ ∂Θ, define the set of unit inward normals 

nΘ(x) := 

> {

ν ∈ Rd : ∥ν∥ = 1 , ⟨ν, y − x⟩ ≥ 0 for all y ∈ Θ

> }

.

For x ∈ int(Θ), set nΘ(x) := ∅.

Definition S.13.1 (Skorokhod problem) . Let υ ∈ D([0 , ∞); Rd) with υ(0) ∈ Θ be given. A pair (ϕ, η sk ) solves the Skorokhod problem with respect to Θ if, for all t ≥ 0, (i) ϕ(t) = υ(t) + ηsk (t) and ϕ(0) = υ(0); (ii) ϕ(t) ∈ Θ; (iii) |ηsk |(t) < ∞;(iv) |ηsk |(t) = ∫ 

> (0 ,t ]

1∂Θ(ϕ(s)) d|ηsk |(s); (v) there exists a measurable function ν : [0 , ∞) → Rd such that ν(s) ∈ nΘ(ϕ(s)) for d|ηsk |-a.e. 

s, and 

ηsk (t) = 

> ∫
> (0 ,t ]

ν(s) d|ηsk |(s).

The path υ is called the unconstrained driving path, while ϕ is the projected path. When the solution is unique, write Γ( υ) := ϕ for its projected component. The following is Dupuis and Nagurney [16, Theorem 1] in the present notation. 86 Theorem S.13.1 (Lipschitz property of the Skorokhod map) . Let Θ ⊂ Rd be a convex polyhedron. For every υ ∈ DBV ([0 , ∞); Rd) with υ(0) ∈ Θ, there exists a unique solution to the Skorokhod problem. Moreover, the corresponding map Γ is Lipschitz, i.e., there exists a constant LSk > 0 such that, for every T < ∞ and every υ1, υ 2 ∈ DBV ([0 , ∞); Rd) with υ1(0) , υ 2(0) ∈ Θ,

sup 

> 0≤t≤T

∥Γ( υ1)( t) − Γ( υ2)( t)∥ ≤ LSk sup 

> 0≤t≤T

∥υ1(t) − υ2(t)∥ .

Whenever a block step path q(m),◦, defined in Section 4, is used as an argument of Γ, extend it constantly beyond the block endpoint: 

q(m),◦(r) := q(m) 

> Km

, r ≥ Hm.

This convention does not affect any estimate on [0 , H m].

Lemma S.13.2 (Unconstrained path for the projected ODE) . Fix τ ∈ R+. There exists an absolutely continuous path w(τ ) : [0 , ∞) → Rdθ satisfying 

w(τ )(0) = z(τ )(0) = θ◦(τ )

˙w(τ )(t) = f (av .)(z(τ )(t); z(τ )(t))

for Lebesgue-a.e. t ≥ 0, such that 

z(τ ) = Γ (w(τ )).

Proof. This follows from the Skorokhod representation of projected dynamical systems in Dupuis and Nagurney [16, Theorem 2].

Theorem S.15.1 (Azuma–Hoeffding) . Suppose {Xk}k≥0 is a scalar martingale and {ck}k≥1 is a deterministic nonnegative sequence such that |Xk − Xk−1| ≤ ck almost surely. Then, for every

Lemma S.15.1 (Well-posedness of the projected ODE) . For every z0 ∈ Θ, the projected ODE 

˙z(t) = Π Θ

(

z(t), f (av .)(z(t); z(t)) 

)

, z(0) = z0,

has a unique solution, and this solution remains in Θ for all t ≥ 0.Proof. Lemma 3.1 gives sup θ∈Θ ∥x⋆(θ)∥ ≤ c8. Hence Assumption 3.5, applied on the ball of radius 

c8, makes the diagonal field F (θ) := f (av .)(θ; θ) Lipschitz on Θ. Define Fext (z) := F (proj Θ z). Nonexpansiveness of projection makes Fext globally Lipschitz; compactness of Θ makes it bounded, and it agrees with F on Θ. Existence follows from Dupuis and Nagurney [16, Theorem 3], while the Skorokhod representation and uniqueness follow from Dupuis and Nagurney [16, Theorem 2]. Hence the projected ODE is well posed and its solution remains in Θ. 

Lemma S.15.2 (Perturbed power-drift recursion) . Let p > 1, κ > 0, ζ > 0, ℓ ≥ 0, and Ce ∈ [0 , ∞).Let {um} and {em} be nonnegative sequences such that um → 0 and, for all sufficiently large m,

um+1 ≤ um − κu pm + em,

and 

em ≤ Ce(m + 2) −ζ {log( m + 2) }ℓ.

Then there exists C < ∞ such that, for all sufficiently large m,

{

(m + 2) −1/(p−1) + ( m + 2) −ζ/p {log( m + 2) }ℓ/p }

.

Proof. Set

and hm := wm + ηm. These sequences are eventually decreasing. Because 1

the mean-value theorem gives, eventually, 0 ≤ wm − wm+1 ≤ Cwwpm.

It also gives 0 ≤ ηm − ηm+1 ≤ Cη

Consequently, for some Ch < ∞,0 ≤ hm − hm+1 ≤ Ch(wpm + ηpm) (S.15.1) eventually. Choose R > 0 so that κpR p−1 ≤ 1/2. Since um → 0 and hm → 0, choose A0 ≥ 1 and then a sufficiently large index M such that

≤ Ah m − AC h(wpm + ηpm)

≤ Ah m+1 = vm+1 .

The map F (u) := u − κu p is nondecreasing on [0 , R ]. Therefore, if um ≤ vm, then 

um+1 ≤ F (um) + em ≤ F (vm) + em ≤ vm+1 .

Induction proves um ≤ vm for every m ≥ M , which is the claimed bound.

# S.16 Step-size shift and the starting index of the analysis 

This appendix describes two ways to ensure that the step-size conditions required by the analysis hold. We can choose N0 sufficiently large and begin the analysis at n = 1, or take N0 = 1 and begin the analysis at a sufficiently large index n0. In both cases, the algorithm runs from n = 1. 

S.16.1 Choosing N0 to begin the analysis at n = 1 

This is the convention already adopted throughout the main paper. We first give a sufficient lower bound on N0 for the analysis to apply from n = 1. For the blockwise fast analysis in Section 6, choose N0 so that (N0 + 1) 1−a ≥ 4.

Equivalently, it is sufficient to take 

N0 ≥

⌈

41/(1 −a)⌉

− 1.

95 The named domination inequality (S.5.1) then holds automatically because Ax ≥ Aθ, Cθ =

Ch, Pois .Aθ, and β1 ≤ α1. The small-step condition is used in the local martingale and Poisson-residual estimates. No additional infinite-horizon condition involving a fixed fast-martingale thresh-old is needed: the block-dependent thresholds will be chosen later so that their failure probabilities are summable. 

S.16.2 Beginning the analysis at n = n0 when N0 = 1 

Alternatively, take N0 = 1 and define 

n0 := min {n ≥ 1 : ( n + 1) 1−a ≥ 4}.

The algorithm runs from n = 1, while the blockwise analysis begins at n = n0, using the iterates generated by the algorithm up to that point. We retain the original iteration index and slow-time grid. For every n ≥ n0,

αn = ( n + 1) −a, βn = ( n + 1) −b, (n + 1) 1−a ≥ 4.

and hence

Beginning the block construction at t(n0) changes only the constants in the block-geometry and geometric-convolution bounds, for both b < 1 and b = 1. Thus, every blockwise result assum-ing (3.3) applies to the blocks beginning at n0, with (S.16.1) providing the required step-size esti-mate. The corresponding almost-sure rates hold for all sufficiently large original indices n. Since 

N0 = 1, these rates are expressed in terms of n + 1, with the same polynomial and logarithmic exponents. 97 S.17 Rates under contraction of the reduced slow map (Proof of Proposition 8.2)

Proof of Proposition 8.2. The proof proceeds in four steps: (i) We identify the projected equilibrium and establish almost-sure convergence of the original iterates under the logarithmically separated step sizes. (ii) We construct Poisson decompositions for the fast and slow noise. For the fast recursion, we form the recursive weighted average Un of the fast noise and analyze pn = xn − Un. For the slow recursion, we construct the projected auxiliary sequence zn and apply Lemma S.17.1 to bound ∥θn − zn∥. These auxiliary sequences are defined in (S.17.10) and are used only in the proof. (iii) We show that the auxiliary slow iterate eventually remains in the face of Θ maximizing the inner product with the averaged drift at the equilibrium. On this face, that drift drops out of the projection objective, yielding a sharper bound on the auxiliary iterate’s increments. (iv) We use fast contraction and reduced slow-map contraction to derive coupled error bounds. An induction gives the rate Oa.s.(log( N0 + n)/√N0 + n), which we then transfer from the auxiliary sequences to the original iterates. 100 We use the original fast norm and Euclidean slow norm. Define the full averaged slow drift and its associated map by 

F (x, θ ) = ∑

> y∈Y

μ(θ)(y)f (y, x, θ ), G(x, θ ) = θ + F (x, θ ).

Thus F (x⋆(θ), θ ) = f (av .)(θ; θ) and G(x⋆(θ), θ ) = G(θ). Constants below are deterministic unless stated otherwise; their values may increase from one estimate to the next. 

Projected equilibrium and initial convergence. The map proj Θ ◦G : Θ → Θ is a contraction, so it has a unique fixed point θ⋆. Put c = 1 − ρs > 0 and v = F (x⋆(θ⋆), θ ⋆). With the outward normal cone convention 

NΘ(θ) = {w : ⟨w, y − θ⟩ ≤ 0 for every y ∈ Θ},

the projection characterization gives 

v ∈ NΘ(θ⋆), θ⋆ = proj Θ[θ⋆ + βv ] (β ≥ 0) . (S.17.7) At a boundary equilibrium, the averaged slow drift v may be nonzero and point outward. Projection nevertheless leaves θ⋆ fixed, as expressed in (S.17.7). Contraction of G implies 

⟨θ − θ⋆, f (av .)(θ; θ) − v⟩ ≤ − c ∥θ − θ⋆∥2 .

Both v and the normal correction in the projected ODE have nonpositive contributions to the derivative of squared distance to θ⋆. Hence its flow satisfies 

∥Stθ − θ⋆∥ ≤ e−ct ∥θ − θ⋆∥ , t ≥ 0. (S.17.8) In particular, the equilibrium set is EQ Θ = {θ⋆}, and distance to θ⋆ is a global Lipschitz Lyapunov function with a uniform one-block contraction. The initial-convergence argument in Section S.7.6, through (S.7.5) and the subsequent slow-block comparison, applies to the present schedule. That part of the argument uses the stand-ing assumptions, the step-size relations, and Lyapunov decrease; it does not use the box-face or common-target conditions. In the present case βn/α n = B/ (A log tn) → 0, tnαn → ∞ , and both step-size sums diverge. The endpoint ratios on fixed fast-time blocks tend to one, while those on fixed slow-time blocks remain bounded. Thus the same Poisson and martingale comparisons, together with (S.17.8), give 

∥xn − x⋆(θn)∥ = Oa.s.

J(θ(t)) > 0, (S.18.8) gives 

ddt J(θ(t)) ≤ − csp J(θ(t)) q−1.

If a trajectory reaches θ⋆, it remains there by uniqueness of the projected flow. Integrating the preceding inequality gives, for each fixed T > 0, 

J(ST (θ)) ≤ J(θ)[1 + csp (q − 2) T J (θ)q−2]−1/(q−2) . (S.18.9) In particular, J is a Lyapunov function, and 

DJ (u) := u − u[1 + csp (q − 2) T u q−2]−1/(q−2) 

is continuous, vanishes at zero, and satisfies 0 < DJ (u) ≤ u for u > 0. Moreover, lim

q > 2, the exponents in (S.18.6) satisfy 1 /2 < a < b < 1. Corollary 8.2 now gives (S.18.7). 106 A family with vanishing curvature. Let 0 ∈ U × V and take

Thus (S.18.2) holds. The Hessians of both penalties vanish at zero, so on sets containing a neighbor-hood of zero neither strong convexity nor strong concavity is available. The proposition also applies when zero lies on the boundary. For example, q = 4 gives a = 6 /11, b = 9 /11, and slow-iterate rate 

Oa.s.(( N0 + n)−1/11 {log( N0 + n)}1/6). These are the rates supplied by the general power-decrease theorem; no optimality claim is made. 

Remark S.18.1 (Comparison with existing frameworks) . The obstruction in these applications is in the reduced slow dynamics; the fast averaged map is contractive. The comparisons below concern the hypotheses of the cited results, rather than possible extensions of their proof techniques. For (S.18.10), suppose 0 is interior to Θ and A̸ = 0. Then 

DF sp (0) = RA := 

> (

0 −AAT 0

> )

.

If σ > 0 is a nonzero singular value of A, the derivative of the forward map θ 7 → θ + hF sp (θ)has eigenvalues 1 ± ihσ , whose moduli exceed one for every h > 0. Projection is inactive in a neighborhood of zero for each fixed h. Hence even the projected forward map is not locally nonexpansive in any fixed norm. This rules out the reduced-map contraction hypotheses of Chandak [6], Chandak et al. [9] and the nonexpansive-map hypothesis of Chandak [5] for this family. The nonconvex-gradient extension in Chandak [5] does not apply either: RA is nonsymmetric, so Fsp 

cannot be the negative Euclidean gradient of a scalar objective near zero. Its minimax specialization, which uses strong concavity in the fast player, also differs from (S.18.5), where the fast variable estimates the field and neither player requires strong curvature. The contractive-map analysis of Chen et al. [10] likewise does not cover this reduced map. The more general drift condition in Chen et al. [11, Assumption 2.2] requires uniform quadratic dissipation toward an unprojected root. Here 

⟨θ, F sp (θ)⟩ = − ∥ u∥q − ∥ v∥q ,

which cannot be bounded above by −c ∥θ∥2 with c > 0 near zero. Nor can a positive-definite quadratic change of Lyapunov function restore local exponential dissipation: the linearization RA

has nonzero purely imaginary eigenvalues. The power-decrease condition (S.18.9) is sufficient for our result. For the actor–critic recursion in Section 9, the lack of a nonexpansive slow map is already visible in a one-state, two-action MDP. Take costs 0 and 1, deterministic return to the same state, Bac = 1, and write p = (1 + e−(ϑ1−ϑ2))−1. At the exact critic, the averaged actor field is 

Fac (ϑ) = (p(1 − p), −p(1 − p)).

Its Jacobian has eigenvalue 2 p(1 − p)(1 − 2p) > 0 in direction (1 , −1) when p < 1/2. At ϑ =(−1/2, 1/2), both this point and ϑ + Fac (ϑ) are interior to the logit box. Thus ϑ 7 → proj Θac (ϑ +

Fac (ϑ)) is not locally nonexpansive in any fixed norm. The same obstruction persists for sufficiently 107 small positive forward steps. At the constrained optimum (1 , −1), the actor field is nonzero and points outward, although the projected velocity is zero. Consequently, neither a reduced-map con-traction/nonexpansiveness condition nor an unprojected-root condition follows from the hypotheses of our actor–critic application. The unprojected slow-gradient result in Chandak [5] does not pro-vide a rate to this constrained equilibrium. In the full application, the controlled Markov sampling and value-based Lyapunov function must also be retained. The actor–critic result concerns precisely the global-clock recursion stated in Section 9, moti-vated by Konda and Borkar [18, Algorithm 3]; it does not extend the present rate claim to the original visit-count or delayed implementation. Neither projection nor Markov noise alone distin-guishes our analysis from all earlier work. The point of these two applications is that projected slow-ODE Lyapunov decrease can hold when the slow-map assumptions in the comparison results fail. 108
